- ▢ **G1 PICK 7 (found 2026-08-03 by measuring, not by reading): the rollback
  SCHEMA is a per-frame budget line, and nothing in the repo treats it as one.**
  The frame-cost probe found a **fixed ~4.3 ms/frame floor** under GGRS —
  snapshot save plus checksum — paid on every frame whether or not a rollback
  occurs, and **1.6× the 2.65 ms cost of simulating the frame at all**.
  ⭐ **that puts two of my own workstreams in direct tension, and I had been
  running only one of them.** The rollback coverage sweep pushes monotonically
  toward registering MORE (its ceiling went 66 → 23 today, and I added three
  resources to the registry). The frame budget pushes the other way: every
  registered entry is serialized every frame, forever, in the shipped Ggrs
  composition. **Registration has been treated as free correctness. It is not
  free, and the sweep has no notion of its own cost.**
  ⚠ **this is NOT an argument to register less** — an unregistered resource that
  should be registered is a desync, which is worse than a slow frame. It is an
  argument that the two need to be *weighed*, and right now only one of them has
  a number.
  ✔ **MEASURED 2026-08-04 — it is FLAT: the cost is per-REGISTRATION, not
  per-entity.**
  ⛔ **AND THE SIGNIFICANCE IS MUCH SMALLER THAN THAT SOUNDS — I first wrote
  "the schema is the lever" and that overclaims.** A later row in this same
  section had already answered the question I was serving: the shipped build is
  `SyncTest(check_distance = 0)`, B9 counted **`SaveWorld` 0× over 120 frames**,
  and both halves of the floor live in `SaveWorld` — **so the shipped game pays
  none of this.** That row had explicitly marked this discriminator LOW PRIORITY,
  *"it can only move the 0.35 ms, and only in a build that saves"*.
  ⭐ **the mistake was procedural, not arithmetic.** The number is right; I
  worked a `▢` without reading the rows AROUND it, and a later row had already
  re-priced it. **Working a stale row and working a row a later row has
  re-prioritised are the same failure** — the fix is the same too: read the
  section, not the line.
  ⚠ **so what the measurement is actually worth**: it tells NETPLAY, where the
  save happens for real, that trimming registrations is the lever and reducing
  entity count is not. That is worth knowing when netplay exists. It says nothing
  about today's frame budget.
  Same rollback settings, two rooms, 300 frames each after 60 of warmup:
  ```
  fixed60 no rollback (lab)   2.58 ms      fixed60 tiny room   2.54 ms
  synctest d=2       (lab)   11.77 ms      synctest d=2 tiny  11.28 ms
  ```
  Rollback overhead: **9.19 ms furnished vs 8.74 ms near-empty — a 5% difference**
  for a whole room's worth of contents. **The cost does not track the entity
  population**, so it is fixed per-REGISTRATION overhead, and by this row's own
  dichotomy the schema is exactly the lever.
  ⚠ **and the floor scales with `check_distance`**, which the original 4.3 ms
  figure did not separate: d=1 → 5.2 ms of overhead, d=2 → 9.2, d=4 → 18.5.
  Roughly linear, as a sync-test resimulating `dist` frames should be.
  ⚠ **caveat, stated rather than buried**: "near-empty" is `tiny_chamber` by
  design, not by a counted entity population. The comparison is between two real
  rooms; it is not a controlled sweep of N.
  ⛔ **and the experiment nearly measured nothing.** My first version varied a
  "body count" argument to `stage_on_floor` — whose parameter is **HP**. It would
  have run four identical populations under four different labels and reported a
  flat result that meant nothing. ⭐ **read the callee's parameter NAMES before
  building an experiment on them**; a knob that does not turn produces the
  cleanest wrong answer there is.
  The original question: Run the same
  probe in a near-empty room against `combat_calibration_lab`. Population-scaling
  means the cost is snapshot volume and the schema is not the lever;
  flat means it is fixed per-registration overhead and the schema is exactly the
  lever. ⛔ do not build either fix before that number exists — that is precisely
  the mistake the LDtk cache above records.
  ⛔⛔ **THE RELEASE NUMBERS ARRIVED AND THEY CUT THIS ROW'S PREMISE DOWN.** Same
  probe, same room, `--release`:
  | fixed 60Hz, no rollback | 2.17 ms/frame |
  | sync-test distance 1    | 4.32 ms/frame |
  | sync-test distance 2    | 6.94 ms/frame |
  | sync-test distance 4    | 11.92 ms/frame |
  **~2.5 ms per resimulated frame over a ~1.8 ms fixed floor** — and the number
  that matters is the comparison I could not make in debug: a resimulated frame
  costs **2.5 ms against a 2.17 ms plain simulation step**, so moving the state —
  save plus restore — is about **0.35 ms.**
  ⭐⭐ **so the cost of a rollback is RE-RUNNING THE SIMULATION, not the schema.**
  The claim I opened this row with — "every registered entry is a per-frame budget
  line" — is true and is roughly a seventh of what I implied. Registering a
  resource is not free, but at 0.35 ms for the entire current schema it is
  nowhere near the lever I called it, and **an unregistered resource that should be
  registered remains strictly worse than a slow frame.** The sweep's direction was
  never in question; my sizing of the counterweight was wrong, by measuring it in
  a debug build and reasoning about a shipped one.
  ✔ **SEPARATED 2026-08-04 by measuring `check_distance` as a sweep**, which is
  the knob the floor's two halves respond to differently:
  ```
  fixed60 no rollback        2.65 ms      synctest dist=1    7.76 ms
  synctest dist=0 (shipped)  1.62 ms      synctest dist=2   11.83 ms
                                          synctest dist=4   20.60 ms
  ```
  **d=0 → d=1 costs +6.14 ms; each further step ~4.2 ms** (+4.07 to d=2, +4.39/step
  to d=4). The steps are pure resim+checksum, so the ~1.9 ms the FIRST step costs
  above the others is the SAVE — paid once when `d > 0` and not at all when it is
  zero. That is the split the row wanted: **save ≈ 1.9 ms, resim+checksum ≈ 4.2 ms
  per unit of distance.**
  ⭐⭐ **and the headline is that `dist=0` is 1.62 ms — FASTER than the
  no-rollback harness at 2.65 ms.** B9's `SaveWorld 0×` census is confirmed by a
  second, independent route: the shipped configuration pays no floor at all.
  ⛔ **but do NOT difference those two numbers.** `fixed60 no rollback` and
  `synctest dist=0` are different COMPOSITIONS, not one composition with rollback
  toggled — a ggrs-hosted frame runs a different schedule from the fixed-tick
  harness, which is exactly why the cheaper one can be the one with rollback
  installed. Subtracting them would produce a negative "cost of rollback" and a
  confident nonsense. **The valid comparisons are within the ggrs family**, which
  is what the step arithmetic above uses.
  ⚠ the original observation stands and is why the split matters: **the checksum
  is sync-test-only**, so a real netplay session pays the save and not the
  compare.
  That also re-explains the 44.3 s test — a large part of its cost is checksum
  work that exists to catch desyncs and has no shipped counterpart at all.
  ▢ **what survives, and it is worth having**: at 60 Hz the budget is 16.67 ms and
  a plain frame is 2.17 ms, so a **6-frame rollback costs ~2.2 + 1.8 + 6×2.5 ≈ 19
  ms and misses the frame.** The constraint is real, it is about simulation
  throughput rather than snapshot size, and **it is now measurable in one command**
  — the probe is kept as `#[ignore]`d instrument
  `probe_what_a_rollback_frame_costs`, with these numbers in its doc comment.
  ✔ **the successor question is ANSWERED for the shipped build, by joining it to
  B9's measurement rather than by a new probe: the floor is ZERO there.** B9
  counted each `bevy_ggrs` schedule by name over a settled 120-frame window with a
  route active — `ReadInputs` 120×, `GgrsSchedule` 120×, `AdvanceWorld` 120×,
  **`SaveWorld` 0×, `LoadWorld` 0×** — because the shipped session is
  `SyncTest(check_distance = 0)`, which never rewinds. Both halves of the 1.8 ms
  floor (the snapshot save AND the checksum) happen in `SaveWorld`. **A frame that
  never saves pays neither.** So "what does the floor split into" does not need
  splitting for today's build; it needs nothing at all.
  ▢ **it survives for NETPLAY and only there**, which is the honest residue: a
  real session saves every frame to make rollback possible, and then the split
  matters — the checksum is sync-test-only and the save is not. Worth measuring
  when there is a session that saves, not before.
  ▢ the population-vs-schema discriminator above is LOW priority for the same
  reason (it can only move the 0.35 ms, and only in a build that saves).

- ◐ **G1 PICK 6: sweep for the OTHER tests that sample the way the two liars did.**
  Two tests lied through their SAMPLING on 2026-08-03 — the Sanic backdrop
  (before/after across a LOOPING course, so the endpoints are the one pair that
  cannot see it working) and the three-point circuit probe (1px tolerance against
  a mover that steps 10px and passes straight through its waypoints). Both cost
  real time; the first cost four wrong diagnoses. So: is the shape anywhere else?
  ⭐ **the hazard is specific, which is what made the sweep cheap**: a before/after
  pair separated by a MANY-FRAME loop, over a subject that can RETURN to its
  starting value. Not every `let before` — only periodic ones.
  ✔ **swept: 41 files use `let before`, 5 have one across a ≥30-frame loop, and
  exactly ONE was a real instance** —
  `fixtures/external_consumer/tests/the_engine_draws_what_it_promised.rs`, which
  is the SAME parallax assertion as the Sanic one, in the outlander. It passes
  today, so it was not lying yet; it has the identical blind spot and would have
  cost the next reader the same four diagnoses. Fixed the same way: track the
  largest deviation across the walk.
  ✔ the other three are safe and it is worth saying WHY, because it is the rule:
  a wallet (monotonic), a rollback frame counter (monotonic), and two fighters'
  summed travel in a brawl (no period). **The trap needs a PERIODIC subject** — a
  looping course, an orbit, a wrap — not merely a before/after pair.
  ⛔ **and no guard was written for it.** Three of five candidates are correct by
  construction and the population is five; a checker here would be a text matcher
  on `let before` that cries wolf on the majority and gets waived on its first
  run. The rule belongs in the reviewer's head and in the two fixed tests'
  comments, which now both say what to check before blaming a system.
  ▢ residue: one stale count fixed in passing —
  `the_stage_kills.rs` said "neither fighter moved in 240 ticks" over a 120-tick
  loop.

- ✔ **G1 PICK 8: the AGGREGATION CONTRACT — DONE (2026-08-04, `7cd0b0f97`).**
  Picked because the authoring-loop program names it in so many words: *"closing
  it needs an aggregation contract — parse each source, merge the schema's own
  fragments, validate the aggregate, lower once — which is the next real piece of
  compiler design, not a handler."* It is the item that makes the next ten
  cheapest: **three unmigrated families are all waiting on this one seam** — boss
  encounters (9 files, half-migrated today), dialogue (7 `.yarn`) and the LDtk
  worlds. Every one of them is many files, one runtime collection.
  **Today's behaviour**: `compile` REFUSES a second lowering source, deliberately
  — "merge semantics is the handler's question and a generic merge would be the
  compiler guessing" — and the fix line in that very refusal is the spec for this
  row. Boss encounters live with it by being `AuthoringOnly`: nine files reach the
  fingerprint and reference resolution, and the runtime **reparses all nine**
  (`BossCatalogFragment::from_prepared_behaviors(.., encounter_rons: &[&str], ..)`
  → `ron::from_str::<BossEncounterSpec>`). Compiler on one path, loading on
  another: the split this crate exists to close, in the crate that closes it.
  **The design, and why this shape**: one trait method with a default, no new
  field on `SchemaRegistration` (24 literals would have to say `Single` for the
  23 that are), and no second "does it aggregate?" predicate that can disagree
  with the merge that implements it.
  ⭐ **the return value IS the declaration** — `Aggregation::{Undefined, Defined}`,
  default `Undefined`, so "this schema has no merge rule" is one fact in one
  place and the compiler asks the handler rather than a registry field.
  ⛔ **it must be called for ONE source too.** The tempting shape is "aggregate
  only when there are ≥2 fragments", and it is wrong for the reason the boss
  encounter table was wrong: a pack that declares a single file would then lower a
  FRAGMENT where the runtime expects the COLLECTION, and the type of the artifact
  would depend on how many files an author happened to write. Silently fine at
  nine, broken at one.
  ⚠ **fragments arrive in DECLARED order** (pack.ron, top to bottom) — a merge
  that overrides needs a defined order, and declared order is the one an author
  can see and diff. Not map order, not filesystem order.
  ⚠ **new `CompileStage::Aggregation`, between facet validation and reference
  resolution.** The pipeline genuinely gains a step, and `stopped_before` has to
  be able to say a refusal there is why nothing downstream ran.
  **Proving it**: the contract is built against a real consumer in the same
  change — `boss_encounter` becomes `Runtime`, lowers one `BossEncounterSpec` per
  file, aggregates to the `BTreeMap<String, BossEncounterSpec>` the catalog
  already holds, and `from_prepared_behaviors` takes that map instead of nine
  strings to reparse. A contract with no caller is a hypothesis, and this repo
  does not build those.
  ✔ **all of the above landed, and the single-source trap was PROBED rather than
  asserted.** The tests were written after the code, so the tempting shortcut
  (`if parts.len() == 1 { pass it through }`) was applied on purpose:
  `an_aggregating_schema_is_asked_to_merge_even_a_single_source` failed and the
  other 23 passed. A guard that has not been seen to fail through its own
  motivating case is a guess.
  ⭐ **the consumer test asserts EQUALITY, not existence.** "The book has nine
  entries" would pass over a catalog builder that ignored the book and reparsed —
  the exact defect being closed. It asserts every catalog entry IS the compiler's
  merged value.
  ⭐ **and it answers half of the program's SECOND limit for free**: aggregation
  is the first point where a schema sees all of its own sources, so "two of these
  nine files claim one encounter id" now has somewhere to live. Cross-SCHEMA
  invariants (exact cover spans the seed library AND the roster) still do not.
  ⛔ **`from_prepared_behaviors` was RENAMED, not supplemented.** It takes
  prepared encounters now as well as a prepared roster, so the old name was a
  lie; `from_prepared` is the name and the one caller moved. A `_v2` beside it
  would have left the repo with two functions and no rule about which to call.
  ▢ **residue — the two families this was built for have not moved yet**:
  dialogue (7 × `.yarn`, and it needs a handler that parses Yarn, which is the
  larger half) and the LDtk worlds. Neither is blocked any more.

- ◐ **G1 PICK 9: MARY-O BECOMES AN LDTK LEVEL — phase 1, the part that needs no
  decision.** Jon, 2026-08-04, direct: *"I would like to make maryo an ldtk level
  so I can manually play with it and lay it out. That means it needs to have the
  right entities exposed for it and make it easy to author in."* Then, before any
  engine change: *"think about what makes this hard vs easy and if an engine
  architecture improvement could make it better and if it can then write a
  proposal document so it can be reviewed before accepting a new architecture
  direction (unless it makes it so easy it's an obvious no brainer)."*
  ⛔ **this OVERRULES the standing "don't propose migrating Mary-O" rule** that
  ADR 0009's table and [[feedback_ldtk_authoring]] both carry. That rule recorded
  a 2026-07-25 statement that demos MAY stay Rust-authored; it was never a ban,
  and Jon has now asked for the opposite for this demo.
  ✔ **the analysis is written and committed**:
  `docs/planning/proposal-authored-vocabulary-2026-08-04.md` (`3005a7be4`).
  ⭐ **the finding, which is the reusable part**: what makes it hard is not LDtk.
  **Mary-O's runtime never reads the level** — `power_block_id(i)` indexes a
  constant array, pipe pairing IS the order of a four-element tuple list, and
  `FlagPole` is a Resource *mirroring* the authored block. Move the blocks and
  keep the Rust and the couplings break **silently**: `power_block_id` names a
  `GeoSource::TileLayer` id and an LDtk entity gets `GeoSource::Placement`, so the
  match simply stops firing.
  ⭐⭐ **the gap underneath: the LDtk pipeline is OPEN at the converter layer and
  CLOSED at the data layer.** `install_ldtk_entity_converters` exists, is
  documented, and is used by NOBODY — because a converter's only output is
  `RoomEmission`, whose channels are all engine-owned, and its one general channel
  carries `PlacementSchema`, a closed six-variant enum. That is also why the
  engine keeps absorbing one game's nouns: the enum grows because it is the only
  place anything can go.
  ⛔ **CORRECTED before Jon read it, and the correction is the valuable part.** I
  first wrote that five concepts were BLOCKED on an engine change, from reading
  TYPE DEFINITIONS — closed enums, no extension slot — without checking what the
  shipped code does about it. **Sanic already authors this exact class in LDtk**:
  its monitor boxes are named `Solid` entities and `break_monitor_boxes` matches
  them by `block.name` (`monitors.rs:119`), with breakage fed back through the
  overlay's `removed_block_names`. **A monitor box IS a ?-block.** Its generator
  even states the rule: monitors stay ENTITY instances so `area create`'s
  static-collision lowering does not eat their names.
  ⚠ **the reusable lesson: a closed enum proves the TYPED channel is closed, not
  that the concept is inexpressible.** Same family as
  [[feedback_ask_the_tool_dont_model_it]] — verify a "blocked" finding against
  the code that ships, by a different route.
  ✔ **so NOTHING here waits on Jon.** The residual gap is real but narrow — an
  authored block carries a NAME and nothing else, so a payload lives in a name
  string — and it is a taste call best made after a week of authoring, not
  before. Choosing an extension mechanism with no second user is how the closed
  enum got closed in the first place.
  ✅ **STEP 1 LANDED** (`1a05b98d7`, `f282e7e2d`) — the row four paragraphs below
  already says so, and this marker disagreed with it. `mary_o.ldtk` exists,
  `level_1_1()` loads it, and both levels are authored areas in it.
  ⛔ **the original text, kept:** terrain as an IntGrid
  collision layer, plus `PlayerStart` / `LoadingZone` / `PickupSpawn` /
  `EnemySpawn` / `Prop`, into
  `game/ambition_demo_mary_o/assets/worlds/mary_o.ldtk`, generated once from
  today's constants and then loaded + grafted the way Sanic already does
  (`game/ambition_demo_sanic/src/lib.rs:14` is the template, and it is a proven
  one). The five special concepts stay Rust-side and keep working untouched.
  ⭐ **IntGrid is not a compromise for the terrain, it is the fix**: an
  IntGrid-derived block carries `GeoSource::TileLayer`, which is what selects the
  TILED art path — an entity-authored surface takes the stretched 128px entity
  texture, which down a 104-tile ground run is exactly the smear that path exists
  to avoid.
  ⚠ **the three exact-layout tests become TRIPWIRES the moment Jon edits the
  file** (`level_1_1_carries_the_grammar_it_claims` and its two siblings). They
  have to become INVARIANTS against the loaded room — every ?-block bonkable over
  reachable ground, every pipe's partner exists, the goal reachable from the
  spawn — which is the version that protects the level and survives being edited.
  ⚠ Mary-O would be `install_ldtk_entity_converters`'s FIRST user, so G3's
  process-global-vs-App-resource endpoint stops being hypothetical. Not fixed as
  part of this; noted so the next reader does not rediscover it.
  ✔ **STEP 1 + 2 LANDED (`1a05b98d7`, `f282e7e2d`).** The file exists, is proved
  equal to the level it replaces (1183 collision cells, zero difference either
  way), and `level_1_1` LOADS it. Nineteen constants and five functions deleted
  rather than kept beside it. `power_block_id(i)` looks its id up in the file
  instead of constructing `GeoId::tile_layer(…, 10 + i)`, and the pipe mouths and
  arrival points read the blocks Jon drew — so moving a pipe moves its trigger.
  ⭐ **eight tests went red and every one was a NAME LOOKUP, which is the
  migration working.** Terrain is painted into an IntGrid and `area create`'s
  lowering eats the name, so the merged rectangles all come back as `ldtk solid`.
  A test that needs the fourth ground run to still be CALLED something is pinned
  to how the level was BUILT, not to what it IS. They ask the collision now —
  contiguous solid at the ground row IS the pit rhythm; the highest surface at a
  column IS the step. That is what they always meant and it survives repainting.
  ⭐ two of them found real things: the vault's coins are authored with LDtk iids
  (`PickupSpawn-106857`), not names, so a `vault_coin_` filter found nothing while
  all eight sat in the chamber; and the coin COUNT stopped being pinned, because
  adding a ninth is authoring rather than a regression.
  ⛔ **JON, mid-run, 2026-08-04 — THE NEXT REQUIREMENT**: *"make sure it is easy
  for authors to write blocks that contain items and to place warp pipes and
  moving platforms."*
  ▢ **this is what decides §4 of the proposal, and it decides it against pure
  name conventions.** Today an author has to type `power_block_3` exactly, a pipe
  pair is a shared name suffix a typo silently breaks, and a ?-block's contents
  are not authorable at all — the reward is a function of her power state in
  Rust, so "a block that contains a mushroom" cannot be said.
  ⭐ **the answer needs no engine change either**, and it is the seam nobody has
  used yet: Mary-O registers its OWN LDtk entity types through
  `install_ldtk_entity_converters` — `MaryOBlock { contains, style }`,
  `MaryOWarpPipe { link, mouth }` — so the AUTHOR fills in fields in the GUI and
  the CONVERTER does the encoding. The name convention stops being something a
  human types and becomes an implementation detail between two pieces of Mary-O.
  ▢ **moving platforms are already standard** (`MovingPlatform` + `KinematicPath`
  converters) and Mary-O uses neither. 1-2's elevators are the first customer —
  Jon has asked for those twice now, in this file and in his observations.
  ▢ and the entity defs have to be REGISTERED in the .ldtk
  (`def register-entity`) or the fields do not appear in the editor at all, which
  is the difference between "authorable" and "authorable by someone holding the
  source".

- ◐ **G1 PICK 10: THE GPT 5.6 REVIEW OF `1a05b98`** — pasted by Jon mid-run,
  2026-08-04. Verbatim source: `docs/planning/review-gpt56-through-1a05b98.md`.
  ✔ **#1 CONFIRMED and FIXED (`2a53dd618`) — and it was MY OWN work from five
  commits earlier.** `canonical_bytes` is built from `define`d rows, and the
  `encounter_waves` handler lowered its whole book and defined nothing. Editing a
  mob's delay, its archetype, or the WAVE ORDER changed what the game runs and
  left the pack fingerprint byte-identical: two peers could carry different
  encounters and agree they had the same content.
  ⭐ **the class is closed, not just the instance**: `compile` now refuses a
  schema that lowers a runtime artifact and defines nothing. ⚠ it checks the
  LINK, not whether the canonical form actually covers the lowered field — no
  compiler check can see that — and the comment says so rather than implying it
  is stronger. The rule caught my own `OrderSchema` toy on its first run.
  ⛔ **probed both ways**: with the `define` removed, the fingerprint test fails
  AND the lowering test fails (the compiler refuses the pack). The probe varies a
  field that is not part of any content id, on purpose — an id change would have
  moved the fingerprint even under the bug.
  ✔ **#3 CONFIRMED and FIXED — and it broke a feature Jon asked for THAT MORNING.**
  `WorldItem.emerging` was set `true` when a ?-block popped a reward and **nothing
  ever set it back**; the field's own doc named a `clear_emerged_powerups` that
  **exists only in that comment**. So a wand finished rising, began its ordinary
  arc, and stayed drawn behind the world for the rest of its life.
  ⭐ the duplicate is DELETED rather than repaired: `ItemMotion::emerging()`
  already compares elapsed rise against the authored one every frame, so SimView
  derives it. **A second mutable copy of a derived fact is a bug with a schedule.**
  ⚠ and the stale comment is what hid it — a reader sees a named cleanup system
  and stops looking for the assignment. That is review finding #8's cost, paid.
  ▢ **#2 roster topology vs session construction** — the review restates the
  ordered-transition remedy. ⚠ **I disproved a NEIGHBOURING remedy from the last
  round by execution** ("compare running settings and restart"), so before
  building this: the four-step transition it asks for is plausible, but check
  what `seat_match_participants`' one-shot gate actually permits first.
  ✔ **#4 dialogue: `Interaction::None` is not a release** — FIXED 2026-08-04.
  The pointer-up signal is the live input state, and it is the same one Bevy's
  own `ui_focus_system` uses to force a pressed node to `None`: a finger came up
  this frame. Observed against `bevy_input` 0.18 rather than assumed — on the
  release frame the finger is already OUT of `Touches::pressed` and present in
  `just_released`, and the mouse reads `just_released` with `pressed` false. So
  `None` activates only on a touch lift; a mouse `None` is always the pointer
  leaving, because a mouse release over the row it pressed arrives as `Hovered`.
  ⭐ **and the review's premise was half the story.** `MenuTapMode`'s default
  returns `Confirmed` from the PRESS for every non-destructive row, so the
  release path governed nothing but an explicitly-configured two-tap mode — the
  fix would have been decorative on every real device. The press now ARMS what
  the policy confirms and the release completes it, which is the split
  `MenuTapMode::default()`'s own comment already argues for: a confirmation
  policy answers "how many taps", drag-cancellation belongs to the gesture layer.
  ⛔ **and `RowPress` was being fed the WINDOW CURSOR on a platform that never
  writes one** — every phone drag passed the 16px test with `origin: None`. It
  reads the finger now.
  ⚠ the stale `effective_dialog_tap_mode` doc comment went too, as this entry
  asked.
  ▢ **#5 Sanic inherits `sandbox_all()`** — carried from the previous round and
  still ⛔ **BLOCKED ON JON**: what Sanic's kit IS is a design call, not a defect.
  ▢ **#6 `--walk` does not gate capture** — cheap, and I wrote the flag. Pick
  CONCURRENT (capture when both counters reach zero) unless the sequential
  reading is what the captures assumed.
  ▢ **#7 process-global installers** — same endpoint as G3, now with a third
  witness. The review explicitly accepts the loud conflict as interim; what it
  asks is that it stay RECORDED as interim rather than counted as done. It is.
  ▢ **#8 stale investigation history in production comments** — accepted in the
  narrow form: a comment that names a system which does not exist is a defect,
  and #3 proves the cost. ⚠ I do NOT accept the general "move dates and model
  names out"; this repo's comments are deliberately archaeological and Jon has
  never asked for that. Trim what CONTRADICTS the code, keep what explains it.
  ⭐ **and the review's own note about my equivalence check is right and I have
  adopted its wording**: it proves collision occupancy and named-block survival,
  not presentation, triggers, metadata or runtime behaviour. The test says so.

- ✔ **G1 PICK 11: GPT 5.6's MARY-O LDTK SPEC** — every section answered. Jon put it at
  `docs/reviews/gpt56-maryo-spec-2026-08-04.md` and pointed me at it mid-run:
  *"there is another to help move the maryo ldtk port forward."*
  ⭐ **it converges with Jon's own mid-run ask** (*"make sure it is easy for
  authors to write blocks that contain items and to place warp pipes and moving
  platforms"*) and with the proposal's §4. Three sources now agree on the same
  next slice, which is a strong signal it is the right one.
  ✔ **§5 FIXED (`5e8ef6fb6`) — the reported bug was real.** A fire-form Mary-O
  bonking a ?-block was swallowed whole: `let Some(reward) = … else { continue }`
  and the ladder answered `None` at the top, so no flinch, no spend, no art
  change, no sound. `next_power_reward` returns a `PowerReward` now, so **there is
  no branch left to swallow** — structural, not a guard.
  ⚠ **the comment over that branch called it "unreachable while the ladder ends
  in the star"; the ladder ends in the BEACON.** Second time in one day a
  confident comment about behaviour that does not exist is what hid a bug.
  ✔ **§6 CONFIRMED, no change needed**: a small Mary-O who collects a lantern
  directly becomes FIRE. `sync_grown_form` derives the form from the beacon
  first, so the modern semantics already hold — deliberately not SMB1, where the
  flower acts as a mushroom when small.
  ✔ **§4 IS DONE — a block is authored by a FIELD.** `MaryOBlock { kind }` is
  registered in the project, so `kind` is a dropdown in the editor; the converter
  encodes it and `block_kind_of` decodes it. No system asks a block for its index
  any more: nineteen constants and every `*_index_for` helper are deleted, and
  `BrokenBricks` went from a `u32` bitset over indices to a `BTreeSet` of authored
  names (ordered, because the overlay iterates it every frame and hash order is
  seeded per process).
  ⛔ **and the slice found a REAL identity bug in my own converter.**
  `Block::solid` leaves the id `GeoSource::Anon` — its doc says so, *"fixture
  constructors default to Anon; the IR emission paths assign real sources"* — so
  every authored reactive block shared ONE identity and a bonk on any of them
  resolved to whichever came first. The engine's `compile_surface` stamps
  `GeoId::placement(iid)` for exactly this reason. A converter that forgets it
  produces geometry with no identity at all, and the migration test now pins that
  every reactive block has a distinct one.
  ⛔ **three TOOLING bugs, all found by running the command rather than reading
  it**: the tool's path to the engine identifier list was three renames stale, so
  `def register-entity` wrote the .ldtk and then died with `FileNotFoundError` —
  a non-zero exit over a file that HAD changed, which is worse than failing. The
  validator's `KNOWN_ENTITIES` is checked BOTH ways (every project must define
  all of it, no instance may fall outside it), so a game's noun could not exist:
  adding it forced every other world to define it, leaving it out made Mary-O's
  own level unsupported. A project that DEFINES an entity has declared it, and
  `--game-owned` keeps a game's noun out of the engine's lists.
  ◐ **§2 enemy placement — the IDENTITY half is done (`b49d968e8`), the
  PLACEMENT half is not.** The spec's *"do not use a human-readable display name
  as gameplay identity"* was a live defect: `tag_mary_o_snakes` and
  `tag_mary_o_ai_slop` matched `FeatureName`, whose own doc says it is *"human-facing
  … for debug overlays / inspectors"*. Renaming the character in the catalog
  would have silently stopped every stomp. They read `FeatureId` now.
  ⭐ **and the app-level test caught the weakness in THAT**, which is the useful
  part: it spawned `id: "scripted_snake"` under a comment claiming it staged a
  snake *"exactly as the level's staging does"* — and it did not, because staging
  mints `mary_o_snake_<i>`. Invisible under a name match, an enemy with no shell
  under an id match. Ids are minted by `snake_id()` / `ai_slop_id()` now, one
  place each, with the contract stated where the predicate lives.
  ⚠ **what I could NOT do is match on the BRAIN**, which is the identity I
  actually wanted: `CharacterBrain::Custom(SNAKE_BRAIN_KEY)` is in the spawn
  request and does not survive onto the entity as a queryable component. So this
  is an id convention held by a helper rather than an identity the entity
  carries — worth revisiting when `ActorSpawn` lands, since the spec wants the
  archetype id to determine brain, body, sprite and dormancy together.
  ✔ **the PLACEMENT half landed too**: the level authors 12 AI Slop and 5 snakes
  as engine-standard `EnemySpawn` entities, the staging reads `spec.enemy_spawns`
  and mints its own stable ids from them, and `AI_SLOP_TILE_COLUMNS`,
  `SNAKE_TILE_COLUMNS`, `stair_slop_spawn_positions`, `STAIR_FIRST_COLUMN`,
  `STAIR_STEPS`, `STAIR_GAP_TILES` and `stair_steps()` are deleted. **1-1 has no
  Rust coordinate sources left.**
  ⚠ dropped at STANDING HEIGHT rather than a measured surface y, so gravity
  settles each onto whatever is under it — an authored y that has to be exact is
  one that breaks when the ground moves.

- ◐ **G1 PICK 12: A FIXTURE COURSE FOR THE PLAYTHROUGH TESTS.** Jon, mid-run,

  ⛔ **he is right and it had ALREADY happened, twice over.**

  `MaryOBlock` and left a `block("power_block_0")` lookup in the app test; the

  found by stashing my work to isolate a different failure. **A suite that went
  green over a test that then fails at HEAD is the thing to understand here** —
  the likeliest reading is that the call sits behind a branch the run did not
  take, which makes it a latent lookup rather than a covered one.
  ✔ **the course LANDED** at `586cb34be` — `test_course.rs`, 40 tiles of unbroken
  ground, one `MaryOBlock`, one snake, a flag; entry chosen by a `MaryOEntryRoom`
  resource read by the prepared-session-world system, so no host plumbing changed

  timing is exactly what went stale. The fixture's whole value is having no
  numbers.
  ▢ **the route across it** is the remaining half — walk, bonk, stomp, goal, on
  the production schedule, driven to CONDITIONS and never to frame counts.
  ▢ *(superseded, kept for the reasoning)* the replacement: a small course owned
  by the test — one pit, one ?-block,
  one snake, a flag — that Jon never authors, so a route can be tuned once and
  stay true. ⚠ it needs the entry room to be parameterisable:
  `mary_o_session_world()` hardcodes `LEVEL_1_1_ROOM_ID` as the entry, so the
  fixture cannot just be a third room in the set.
  ▢ **what is uncovered until then, stated plainly**: the end-to-end run. The
  mechanics are covered against the authored level by unit probes (bonk, stomp,
  brick, warp) and 1-1's shape by invariants, but nothing currently walks her
  spawn-to-flagpole on the production schedule. Mary-O
  registers its OWN LDtk entity types through `install_ldtk_entity_converters`
  (the seam nobody has used yet) so an author fills in FIELDS in the GUI and the
  converter does the name encoding. `MaryOBlock { kind, contains }` and an
  `ActorSpawn`-shaped enemy placement keyed by stable archetype id — **never by
  display name**, which the spec calls out and which Mary-O's snake/stomp systems
  currently do.
  ⚠ **it forces the index→id rekey I deferred.** A converter sees one entity and
  cannot know an ordinal, so `power_block_index_for(id) -> Option<usize>` becomes
  a kind lookup, and `BrokenBricks(u32)`'s bitset — ROLLBACK STATE — has to be
  keyed by `GeoId` instead of by a dense index. That is the real cost of this
  slice and it is worth paying once.
  ◐ **§3 transitions (RoomAnchor / RoomExit, pipe destinations, victory as an
  OUTCOME rather than a loading zone)** — the largest item and the one I would do
  last. ✔ the room-to-room half landed 2026-08-05 with 1-2's authoring:
  `descent_to_1_2`/`surface_return_from_1_2` were the last derived geometry in
  1-1 and are authored zones now, and the links come from their own
  `target_room`/`target_zone`. ✔ "the flag replays this room" stopped being
  hard-coded at `7b67014c5` (`LevelDestination`). ▢ what remains is PIPE
  destinations and victory-as-an-outcome.
  ▢ **§1's endpoint: the generator must stop being destructive.**
  `author_mary_o_ldtk.py` deletes and rebuilds the project from mirrored Rust
  constants — correct for bootstrapping, fatal the moment Jon edits the file. It
  has to split into schema/definition sync (safe to re-run) and level generation
  (never re-run after authoring begins). **This is the highest-risk item on the
  list**: the next person to run that script casually destroys Jon's work.
  ⛔ and I accept the spec's boundary without argument: **power forms, wand and
  lantern semantics, reward selection, damage downgrades, flag and pipe
  choreography stay in the Mary-O crate.** LDtk authors WHICH block and WHERE;
  Rust decides what it means. No engine-interpreted progression language.

- ✔ **G1 PICK 13: THE GPT 5.6 REVIEW OF `90a9715`** — all three fixed. Three findings, pasted by
  Jon mid-run 2026-08-04. Its own priority order, kept.
  ✔ **#1 EVERY LDTK ENEMY IS CONSTRUCTED TWICE — CONFIRMED, FIXED at `07f0fc7cc`.** Not
  argued from types: a probe booted the real app and dumped every `FeatureId`.
  1-1's **17** authored `EnemySpawn` placements produce **34** actors — 17 bare
  `EnemySpawn-1068xx` named `EnemySpawn`, and 17 `mary_o_ai_slop_EnemySpawn-…` /
  `mary_o_snake_EnemySpawn-…` named `AI Slop` / `Solid Snake`. Two roots:
  the engine's `authored_actor_requests()` builds one row per `room.enemy_spawns`
  entry UNCONDITIONALLY, and Mary-O's `RoomContentStagingRegistry` closures
  (`snake_spawn_requests`, `ai_slop_spawn_requests`) iterate the SAME entries and
  mint a second actor under a prefixed id.
  ⚠ **the ids differ, so the plan's duplicate-ID check never fires** — the guard
  that exists for exactly this cannot see it, because the two paths disagree
  about what to call the same authored thing.
  ⚠ **and it is worse than double population.** Only the prefixed copy is tagged:
  `is_ai_slop_id` / `is_snake_id` match an id PREFIX, so the generic copy is a
  custom-brain enemy with no `AiSlop`/`SnakeShell`, no stomp, no shell, no
  dormancy policy. Half of 1-1's enemies are un-stompable lookalikes.
  ⭐ **the regression is the LDtk port itself**: before it, 1-1 authored NO
  `enemy_spawns` and staging was the only root. Authoring them gave the generic
  path something to build, and nothing removed the closure.
  ⛔ **do not fix by suppressing duplicate ids** — GPT is right that the two roots
  must not both exist. Keep the generic path (`EnemySpawn` is already an engine
  placement), delete the Mary-O staging closures, and make the tag pass key off
  **stable archetype identity the placement already carries** rather than an id
  prefix minted by the path being deleted. That is the same endpoint as the
  carried row *"use stable archetype/content identity for enemy-specific
  behavior instead of display names"* — this finding just makes it urgent.
  ✔ **#2 `sync_mary_o_ldtk_defs.py` did not synchronize — FIXED at `63dd71844`.** It shells out to `def
  register-entity`, which refuses an identifier that already exists; the wrapper
  matches that error text and prints success. So after the first run, editing
  `mary_o_entities.json` changes nothing — not fields, types, defaults,
  descriptions, dimensions — and says it worked. Either implement a real upsert
  (preserve the def UID, update field defs by stable identifier, preserve
  compatible instance values, report incompatible changes) or rename it to what
  it does. ⭐ the second is honest and cheap; the first is what §1 actually needs.
  ✔ **#3 validation treated every project-defined entity as runtime-supported — FIXED at `11b74dbe7`.**
  Letting `MaryOBlock` through by accepting anything in `defs.entities` was the
  wrong seam: an editor definition does not prove a converter was registered.
  ⚠ **GPT REPRODUCED this** — a `BogusEntity` def + instance passes validation
  with no converter anywhere. The fix is an explicit game-extension vocabulary
  (engine-known entities **+** declared game-owned converter ids), not
  "present in the file".
  ⭐ **all three share one shape**: a check that cannot fail, because the thing it
  compares against is derived from the thing being checked.

  ⭐ **what the #1 fix cost and taught, recorded because both were measured:**
  the surviving actor's body box comes from the AUTHORED rectangle, not the Rust
  constant the deleted request passed — a snake spent two frames at 14x16 before
  `SpritePosedBody` corrected it to the sheet's 29.25x13. Both tag passes set the
  box when they tag, which takes that to ONE frame, and frame one is the spawn
  frame itself: the floor without the engine consulting the demo. The rule this
  settles is worth keeping: **an enemy's authored rectangle says WHERE, not HOW
  BIG.**
  ⚠ **a game crate cannot build an `ActorConfig`** — no `Default`, and its
  `tuning`/`brain_spec` come from an `ArchetypeSpec` the umbrella does not
  re-export (the E9 oracle). That forced the AI Slop dormancy unit test into the
  app crate. It got stronger for it — the old fixture could only prove the pass
  answers something a test invented — but the ergonomic gap is real and is a
  candidate row of its own if another demo hits it.

    files consume them. Making the catalog App-local either changes all of them
    to owned strings/non-static borrows, or requires threading provider context
    into an enum method that deliberately has none.

    provider rather than replacing it, which kills the actual defect (two games
    in one process silently share the FIRST one's items) without touching the
    read API — but only if a reader can learn which provider it is asking for,
    and today it cannot. That is the question to answer before writing code.

    BECAUSE the storage is global — at 59 sites across 16 files. The wave book

- ✔ **G1 PICK 16: THE SR OVERLAY** — Jon handed over a GPT 5.6 overlay zip on
  2026-08-04 and asked for it applied and working. Landed at `71a40dda0`: two new
  crates (`ambition_relativity`, `ambition_relativity2d`), the TwinTrack demo and
  its app, ADR 0011 amended, rollback schema v6 → v7. Every one of its fifteen
  edits to existing files was purely additive against the current tree, so it
  applied clean; the work was the three things that stopped it RUNNING — an
  inline `Rollback` marker that panics without a live ggrs session, an
  unaccounted mutable resource in the shipped composition, and a prelude glob the
  no-warnings gate refused. ⭐ **the determinism note is the part worth keeping**:
  the rollback path's only transcendental is `sqrt`, which IEEE-754 pins, and the
  rapidity helpers (`atanh`/`tanh`, libm, not bit-identical across platforms) are
  unused by the sim — so composing velocities inside the sim would be an ADR 0023
  decision rather than a function call. Written into the math, not left implicit.

- ▢ **G1 PICK 15: MARY-O LDTK POLISH — JON'S REQUIREMENTS, VERBATIM (2026-08-04).**
  Recorded before acting, because the rule is that his answers get written down
  first and this list has five distinct asks in one paragraph:

  > *"make sure the maryo ldtk is polished. It should be possible to spawn a
  > block that looks like a brick but really has a powerup. We should also allow
  > for bricks to have explicit items. E.g. always a wand, always a lantern, or a
  > level-towards lantern powerup. In the future we could level towards something
  > else (e.g. buble flowers or other maryo pickups, so leave that seam open).
  > The pipe placements need to be linkable with a better schema (maybe similar
  > to how we do portals), and we need to build a real world 1-2 that you go to
  > when you complete world 1-1. So the end goal needs to be able to specify
  > where the level transitions to. The end of 1-2 should transition back to 1-1.
  > Triage that for after you finish getting the relativity patch in and working.
  > You should probably audit it and fix any issues if you see any too."*

  Broken out, in the order that makes the next ones cheapest:
  ✔ **APPEARANCE and CONTENTS become independent** — `1ce3be0bc`. A block that LOOKS like a
    brick and holds a powerup is the case that proves it: today `MaryOBlockKind`

    contains nothing". Split it: what it looks like, and what it holds.
  ✔ **A block's contents are AUTHORED and EXPLICIT** — `Empty`/`Always(p)`/`Toward(p)`. Always a wand, always a
    lantern, or "level towards lantern" (the current implicit behaviour, which
    becomes one option among several rather than the only rule).
  ✔ ⭐ **the ladder is OPEN**, in Jon's words: *"in the future we could level
    towards something else (e.g. bubble flowers or other maryo pickups)"*. So the
    contents field is not a closed three-variant enum — it names a pickup, and

    solves exactly this shape in LDtk (an entity ref between two placements) and

    (2026-08-05). It existed as a real Rust level reached by the vault shortcut
    and had a goal and a two-way link with 1-1 (`7b67014c5`); what it was not was

    parsed trees and enumerating differences — every file differs by exactly

- ◐ **G1 PICK 17: THE SESSION IS SIZED BY WHOEVER WINS A RACE.** The carried row
  said *"publish the decided roster topology before rollback-session
  construction"*. **CONFIRMED by execution, and it is worse and better than the
  row implies.**
  ⭐ **the two shipped routes size the ggrs session from two different
  authorities**, and which one wins is an unordered `Update` race:
  `maintain_local_session` (`rollback/local_session.rs:155`,
  `LocalSessionSet::Maintain`) captures a DEVICE-derived topology and builds the
  session from the pad count; `freeze_local_seating_for_the_decided_match`
  (`schedule/input_systems.rs:1452`, `InputSet::Collect`) publishes the
  ROSTER-declared one. Nothing orders them. Both are in `Update`, so this is a
  real intra-schedule ambiguity and NOT the vacuous-cross-schedule-`.after` trap.
  ▢ **measured, versus route**: frame 2 the maintainer froze `declared=None`,
  `players=1`, and built ggrs with 1 handle; frame 3 the roster's
  `declared=Some(2)` arrived one frame late and the session is never rebuilt —
  the comparison was deliberately removed (`local_session.rs:218-229`).
  ▢ **measured, smash route**: the roster wins, and the session gets 2 handles
  for ONE human because `declared_seats = roster.participants.len()` counts the
  CPU seat.
  ⚠ **no dead seat TODAY**, and the reason is alarming rather than reassuring:
  `reconcile_roster_with_frozen_topology` would rebuild versus' roster as TWO
  HUMAN seats against a ONE-handle session — seat 1 with no device and no ggrs
  handle, whose frames `publish_local_inputs` drops, which is exactly the
  *"seat two authored 40 frames of right and its fighter moved 0.00px"* failure.
  It does not fire only because `roster.seat_topology == Some(generation)`
  early-returns, and both topologies happen to carry generation **1**.
  ⛔ **THE GENERATION COLLISION IS LOAD-BEARING IN THE WRONG DIRECTION, so ORDER
  OF FIX MATTERS.** `freeze_local_seating_for_the_decided_match` builds a fresh
  `LocalSeatTopology::default()` and counts from 0 → 1, colliding with the
  maintainer's gen 1 — and `generation` exists precisely so a consumer can notice
  a rebuild. Fixing the collision FIRST would expose the two-human rebuild.
  **Size first, then generation.**
  ✔ **PART 1 of 3 landed** (`b348d44f4`): `declared_seats` counts HUMANS, not
  participants. Both readers wanted that — the ggrs handle count and the
  solo-vs-couch branch — so a one-human-one-CPU smash match no longer builds a
  spare handle, nor puts a solo player on the positional-pad branch where a pad
  at any index but 0 gives them nothing. ⚠ the test that pinned the old count
  could not simply be retuned: with one human and no pads the roster count and
  the device count are both 1, so that fixture stopped being able to separate
  them at all. It now asserts what only it can — that a CPU does not inflate the
  count — and asserts `humans < declared` first so it cannot quietly stop
  distinguishing the two.
  ✔ **PART 2 landed** (`0db30ebda`): the `Maintain.after(InputSet::Collect)`
  edge. ▢ **the maintainer still does not WAIT** for a roster-declared topology —
  a session that starts before any roster is published sizes itself from devices,
  which is right for a host with no match to decide and wrong for one whose
  roster lands a frame later. Closing that needs the maintainer to know a roster
  is COMING, which nothing tells it. That is the residue.
  ⚠ **and "make versus publish earlier, like smash does" is NOT available** —
  checked. Smash has a character-select screen that decides a roster and THEN
  navigates, so it publishes before the `GoTo`. Versus has no select screen:
  `track_versus_roster` fires on `on_versus && !mine`, deriving the roster from
  the device count on the frame the route becomes active. Its roster IS a
  function of arriving, so there is no earlier moment to move it to.
  ⭐ **the shape of the real answer**, for whoever takes it: sizing a session is a
  decision about WHO IS PLAYING, which is the roster's job — so the maintainer
  arguably should not capture devices at all, and should size from
  `declared_seats` or from one local player. ▢ what stops that being obviously
  right is a couch session with two pads and no roster, and whether such a thing
  exists is the question to settle first. Do not guess it.
  ✔ **PART 3 landed** (`0db30ebda`), and it became SAFE because part 1 landed
  first: counting humans made `versus_roster_from`'s own parameter name true
  (`local_players`), so the rebuild the collision was suppressing now produces
  Human + Cpu instead of Human + Human. Measured across the change — before,
  gen 1 then gen 1 with the roster ignored; after, gen 1 then gen 2 with the
  reconciler running and building the right roster.
  ⚠ **still unknown**: every probe ran with ZERO pads. The keyboard-plus-one-pad
  smash case is the one most likely to expose human-seats > handles.
  ⚠ **and the neighbouring carried row is this row.** *"Store and compare the
  COMPLETE running session settings"* — they ARE stored
  (`RollbackSessionOwnership::LocalSyncTest { settings }`); the comparison was
  removed on purpose because restarting a live session loses the seat→handle
  binding. That item cannot be closed by "compare", so it is folded in here.
  ▢ **stale docs that will mislead the next reader**: `versus.rs:366-370` still
  says a frozen topology *"today is a `dev_tools` build"* — false since the
  match-freeze shipped and since the maintainer also captures, which is what
  makes the two-writer problem reachable in every build.
  `crates/ambition_platformer2d/src/rollback.rs:158-171` repeats the claim.
  ⚠ **what is NOT known**: whether the observed order is stable (all probes ran
  with ZERO pads; the keyboard-plus-one-pad smash case is the one most likely to
  expose human-seats > handles and was not exercised).

  > *"the enemy sprites are red squares. They are not getting art."*

  > *"if there is a way to refactor the brick system to make it more elegant
  > consider that."*

  - ✅ **red-square enemies** (`d72da7a3b`). Mine: `07f0fc7cc` deduped the double
    build and deleted the only writer of the display NAME with it. The render
    binder resolves art by name, so all 17 bound as `"EnemySpawn"` and drew the
    hostile placeholder. Restored where Sanic does it. ⭐ the guard is the
    keeper — `binding_tests` skipped the room sweep because *"Mary-O authors no
    enemy spawns, so a room sweep would be an assertion that cannot fail"*, a
    premise the LDtk migration expired and nobody revisited.
  - ✅ **block state survives death** (`c437d17b5`). A death emits
    `RoomReplayRequested`; both refills read `RoomLoaded`, which is written from
    exactly one place — an actual room load. `ContentRoomReplayResetSet` is the
    engine's declared slot for content-named per-attempt state and Mary-O had
    never reached for it. Second defect in the same two lines: both were gated
    on `room_id == LEVEL_1_1_ROOM_ID`, so 1-2's blocks would never have rearmed
    at all.
  - ✅ **the black screen across a level transition** (`632ecf1b4`). Not a
    stall — a literal opaque cover node, held to its 8-second give-up deadline.
    It waits for zero `UnclaimedBodyPlaceholder`s, and a stand-in was SPAWNED
    from `FeatureViewIndex` (every feature) but RETIRED only via
    `DynamicFeatureViews` (the discovered ones), so one drawn for an AUTHORED
    brick after a room commit was unreachable by both cleanup paths. The
    system's own doc already warned about "the diagnosis outliving the bug it
    diagnosed"; the guarantee held for one of two populations. ⚠ the ordering
    hole under it is NOT fixed and must not be papered over —
    `respawn_room_visuals_on_request` is in `Update`, this chain is in a sim
    phase, and a cross-schedule `.after` is silently vacuous. Cost is now one
    frame of magenta instead of eight seconds of black. **Own row below.**
  - ✅ **the flag that does not end the level** (`e066ec6d1`). Not the quasar and
    not the approach: all three forms are the same width, the star never writes
    `ScriptedControl`, and the flag runs after the star chain so it has the last
    word on the pose. The defect is a CONTRACT mismatch — content wrote
    `RoomTransitionRequested` once and reset to `Idle` in the same call, and
    every other producer is a zone that re-emits every tick, which is what the
    transaction's failure paths silently assume. Mary-O follows that contract
    now, holds the tally until the room actually changes, and gives up loudly
    after 8s. ⚠ NOT the same defect as review #1 — that one is still open and is
    about rollback, not about this.
  - ✅ **two `MaryOBlock`s stacked in one cell** in 1-1 at px `[1600, 288]`, a
    Quasar on top of the third Brick of a three-brick row — which one a bonk
    resolves depends on block order. ⭐ the intent is unmistakably Jon's own
    ask: a brick that CONTAINS a quasar. Folded into the block-schema row below
    rather than fixed by deleting one, because deleting one throws away what the
    author meant.
  - ▢ **brick texture, cammo/hidden reward blocks, and coin blocks.**
    ⛔ **RETRACTED, an hour after I wrote it:** this row said the `.ldtk` could
    only author a single-axis `kind` and that the two-axis schema had to be
    built. It is already there — `MaryOBlock`'s entity def carries BOTH `kind`
    and an optional `contents`, and `convert_mary_o_block` already reads it. I
    inferred the field's absence from the instance data (every placement authors
    `kind` and none authors `contents`) instead of reading the def. Asking the
    file would have taken one command; modelling it produced a confident, wrong
    plan for a migration that was already done.
    So **"cammo" needs no work at all** — `kind: Brick, contents: Always<Quasar>`
    says it today, which is also the shape the stacked pair should have been.
    What is GENUINELY missing, and is the whole of this row:
    - ✅ an INVISIBLE look (`Hidden`) — `54349c137`. Transparent through the same
      seam the pipes use, so only the paint goes; the box stays where the author
      put it, because you find a hidden block by jumping into it. Reveals itself
      by being used (its only art is the spent tile).
    - ✅ a `Coin` reward that credits WITHOUT spawning a world item —
      `54349c137`. `BlockPayout::{Item, Coins}`: expressing a coin as a
      `PowerReward` would have meant inventing a fake item and teaching the
      spawner to skip it. Credits the same `BodyWallet` the vault's loose coins
      do. ⚠ neither is placed in a shipped level yet — the capability is tested,
      the LEVEL does not exercise it. Placing a solid-but-invisible block is a
      spatial-review job, and blind placement can wall off a route.
    - the breakable brick sharing the level's masonry texture. ⭐ **located:**
      `dress_power_blocks` attaches `BlockArt` only for the `Question` and
      `Quasar` looks and skips `Brick` entirely, so a brick falls back to the
      `BlockKind::Solid` default — `entities/solid_block.png`, a plain dark
      slab — while the level's ground is an IntGrid TILE LAYER drawing its
      tileset. They are two different pipelines, which is why they cannot match
      by accident. ⚠ this one needs an ART ASSET, not a mapping: there is no
      brick-masonry `EntitySprite` to point at, so the fix is to generate one
      that matches the terrain tile (the sprite renderer owns that) and then add
      the `Brick` arm to the dresser. Do not "fix" it by reusing `SolidBlock`.
      ⛔ **I said twice that the brick "draws as a wooden crate", once to Jon.
      It does not.** The tan crates in every Mary-O capture are SNAKES in their
      withdrawn state — `boxed_idle`, the shell you kick.
      ⭐ **LOOKED AT, finally**, once `capture_mary_o --at` existed to put the
      camera on them: 1-1's three bricks draw as `entities/solid_block.png`, the
      generic dark slab with a 2x2 cross — the `BlockKind::Solid` fallback,
      because `dress_power_blocks` handles `Question`/`Quasar` and skips `Brick`.
      ✅ **fixed, and it needed no art.** `EntitySprite::SolidTile` already
      exists and already IS the seamless brick pattern — the level's IntGrid
      surfaces draw it. Entity-derived blocks fell back to `SolidBlock` while
      IntGrid-derived ones got `SolidTile`: two spawn paths, one
      `BlockKind::Solid`, two textures, and that asymmetry was the whole defect.
      One `Brick` arm in `dress_power_blocks`. Verified by capture at
      `--at 1568,288 --no-ui`, before and after.
  - ▢ **an LDtk validator rule for two entities in one cell.** Nothing caught
    the stacked pair; it was found by a subagent walking the parsed tree. A
    level file that can express "two blocks in the same 32px cell" will do it
    again.
  - ▢ **elegance pass on the brick system**, explicitly invited. Consider it

  ordering between them at all. `draw_unclaimed_feature_views`'s own doc claims
  it "runs AFTER every family's own spawn": true within its chain, false against
  the room-load spawner. ⛔ **do not fix with `.after`** — that edge crosses

  change nothing. The question is which schedule the room-visual respawn belongs
  in, and that is an architecture decision, not a one-liner.

- ✅ **G1 PICK 29 (WAS A SECOND "PICK 17"): GPT 5.6 REVIEW THROUGH `f0f97f5`.**
  ⚠ **renumbered 2026-08-05 — two different rows carried the number 17**, this
  one and the session-sizing race at the top of this section, and three
  cross-references above ("see G1 PICK 17", "the two-writer problem in PICK 17")
  all mean the SESSION-SIZING row. A reader following them landed here half the
  time. Numbers are navigation, and a duplicate one is a broken link that no link
  checker can see.
  All five findings below are resolved — four fixed, one refused with evidence —
  so the header marker was stale too. Five findings,
  his priority order kept.
  1. ✅ **Mary-O's goal resources are not rollback-derived in time** — confirmed
     and fixed. `follow_the_active_room` moved out of `Update` and into the sim
     `rules` chain, immediately ahead of the two systems that read what it
     writes. ⚠ **VERIFICATION GAP, stated rather than papered over**: this is a
     schedule move, and nothing in the suite drives a ggrs rewind across a Mary-O
     room transition, so the fix is argued from the ordering (now explicit and
     chained) rather than demonstrated. A behavioural test in the rules-only
     harness CANNOT discriminate — `sim` and `Update` both run on `app.update()`
     there, so such a test would have passed against the defect too, and writing
     it would only have looked like coverage. The real probe needs a rollback
     fixture. ⭐ note the derive is cheap to run per-tick because its `Local` memo
     compares against the ROOM: the expensive re-answer happens only on a room
     change, and a rewound room disagrees with a memo from the future, which is
     exactly when it should.
     ⛔ **original finding, kept:** `FlagPole`
     and `LevelDestination` are declared rollback-derived but rebuilt by
     `follow_the_active_room` in ordinary `Update`, while `run_flag_sequence` and
     `cycle_level_on_flag_tally` consume them in the sim schedule. A rollback
     across a room transition resimulates 1-1's ticks against 1-2's pole and
     destination, and the `Local<Option<String>>` memo does not rewind either.
     ⭐ the honest fix is to stop duplicating the room's own data into resources
     and resolve the goal from `RoomSet` at the point of use — an `Update`-only
     projection is presentation, not simulation input.
  2. ✅ **TwinTrack reset through the authority** — both writes, not just the
     flagged one: the completion STOP was a bare `vel = ZERO` too, which leaves
     the collapsed sweep still describing the inbound run. Each is now one
     `transit_body(.., TransitVelocity::Zero)`.
     ⭐ **the finding under the finding: the guard could not see it.**
     `engine.pose-writes-are-authority-only` and its velocity sibling match the
     receiver's NAME — `kin.` and `kinematics.` — not its type. TwinTrack bound
     a `BodyKinematics` as `body` and walked past both. Widened to ` body.`
     (leading space: bare `body.pos` also matches the tail of `player_body.pos`,
     which made `camera_snapshot.rs` two false positives on the first run), and
     the blind spot is now written into the rule's own rationale rather than
     left implied. ⚠ **it is still a name check** — a fourth identifier passes
     unseen, and the real fix is a naming contract on `BodyKinematics` bindings
     or a type-aware pass. Probed both ways.
  3. ✅ **the capture's walk boundary, both binaries.** GPT had the direction
     right and the mechanism half right: he said the input layer *had already*
     collected, but `InputSet::Collect` is in `Update` too and these systems had
     NO edge to it — so whether a press was seen this frame or the next was
     whatever order Bevy happened to pick. Ambiguity in the instrument that
     settles arguments is worse than a known offset. Now ordered
     `.before(InputSet::Collect)`, plus a one-frame `settle` so the last walking
     press is simulated before the shutter. Sanic's copy fixed identically.
  4. ✅ **the source archive and overlay zip were tracked** (`0eb4c9d0d`).
     Confirmed: `286f5f0cf` untracked them and the merge `c8a32cf93` brought all
     12 straight back. ⭐ the finding under the finding: BOTH ignore rules were
     written against filenames that do not exist —
     `.tmp-ambition-agent-source-*/` (the snapshots carry no `.tmp-` prefix) and
     `/*_overlay_*.zip` (every zip Jon drops is hyphenated). Neither had ever
     matched a single file.
  5. ⛔ **REFUSED — trailing whitespace at 8702/9136 is LDtk's OWN format.** Both
     lines are six tabs and nothing else, the indent LDtk writes before closing a
     multi-line `intGridCsv` array. Two independent routes say so: `sandbox.ldtk`
     — authored in the editor, untouched by any of this work — carries twelve of
     the identical line, and re-running `editor_format` on `mary_o.ldtk`
     reproduces exactly two. Normalising it would fight the editor: the next time
     Jon saves, LDtk puts it back, and the file that exists to be diffable stops
     matching what the authoring tool emits. `editor_format` exists precisely to
     preserve this. Not a defect; a correct reading of a file the reviewer could
     not run the tooling against.

  ⚠ he could not run Cargo or the game, so every Rust claim above is unverified
  by execution; #1 in particular asserts a schedule relationship that must be
  read in the code before it is believed.
